At a height outside $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(z)$, the finite representative of $z$ instead gives $T(k)\wedge\Phi^Kz=0$, by [prop:finite-local](#prop:finite-local). Thus every retained telescope kills $\Phi^K(z\otimes \mathcal A_B)$. This spectrum is $L_m^f$-local, so it is zero. Forgetting a coherent action detects zero, which proves <a href="#eq:outgoing-orthogonality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:outgoing-orthogonality">[eq:outgoing-orthogonality]</a>.

For $J\not\sim B$, every summand of $\Phi^B\mathcal A_J$ comes from a proper normal subgroup $J'\sim J$ of $B$. Since $\ell(B/J',1)>0$, at $k=m$ kills that summand after smashing with $T(m)$. All smaller-height tests are killed by the $z$-factor. The same detection argument proves <a href="#eq:incoming-orthogonality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:incoming-orthogonality">[eq:incoming-orthogonality]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-thm-specialization"></a>
<div id="thm:specialization" class="theorem">

**Theorem 28** (Relative specialization bound). *For a finite $p$-group $G$, a subgroup $H\le G$, and $m,n\ge0$, $$(H,m)\in\overline{\{(G,n)\}}
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 m\ge n+\ell(G,H).$$ The closure is taken in the tensor-triangular spectrum of finite genuine $p$-local $G$-spectra.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-thm-specialization"><summary>Proof of stmt-thm-specialization</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $G,n$ and put $y=(G,n)$. Any specialization $(H,m)$ of $y$ has $m\ge n$. Indeed, if $m<n$, inflate a finite spectrum of exact type $m+1$. It belongs to $\mathcal P_m(H)$ and not to $\mathcal P_n(G)$, contradicting <a href="#eq:prime-closure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prime-closure">[eq:prime-closure]</a>.

Suppose that the theorem fails. Choose the least violating height $m$ and a violating subgroup $B$ at that height. Thus $$(B,m)\in\overline{\{y\}},\qquad m-n<\ell(G,B),$$ and $B\ne G$. By [prop:finite-local](#prop:finite-local), the spectrum $X_m=\mathop{\mathrm{Spc}}((\mathcal C_m^G)^c)$ is a finite subspace containing $y$ and $(B,m)$. Let $Z=\overline{\{y\}}\cap X_m$, the closure of $y$ in that subspace.

We construct one compact object with support exactly $Z$, keeping a finite genuine representative. For every $q\in X_m\setminus Z$, reverse prime inclusion gives $q\not\subseteq y$. Choose a finite genuine spectrum $a_q\in q\setminus y$. Its support contains $y$, hence all of $Z$, and excludes $q$. The finite tensor product $$a=\bigotimes_{q\in X_m\setminus Z}a_q,\qquad z=e\otimes a$$ (with the unit as empty product) satisfies $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(z)=Z$. It is compact and dualizable, and its displayed finite representative allows us to apply [lem:layer-orthogonality](#lem:layer-orthogonality).

Minimality of $m$ shows that every $(K,k)\in Z$ with $k<m$ satisfies $\ell(G,K)\le k-n$. Therefore [lem:layer-orthogonality](#lem:layer-orthogonality), [lem:layer-splitting](#lem:layer-splitting) give $$z\simeq X\oplus Q,\qquad
 Q=F_B\phi_Bz,\qquad X\otimes Q=0,$$ with both summands compact, $\phi_BX=0$, and $\phi_KQ=0$ for $K\not\sim B$. Their compact supports are disjoint closed subsets whose union is $Z$. Both are nonempty: $(B,m)\in\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(Q)$, since $\phi_BQ\simeq\phi_Bz$, and $y\in\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(X)$, since $B\ne G$ and $\phi_GQ=0$. This contradicts irreducibility of the closure of a point. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-thm-specialization-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-thm-specialization</summary>

*Proof of [thm:main](#thm:main).* For an integer $r\ge0$, the universal implication defining $r_n(G,H)$ is precisely $$\mathcal P_{n+r}^G(H)\subseteq\mathcal P_n^G(G).$$ By <a href="#eq:prime-closure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prime-closure">[eq:prime-closure]</a> and [thm:specialization](#thm:specialization), this inclusion forces $r\ge\ell(G,H)$. Equivalently, for every smaller $r$, its failure supplies a finite genuine spectrum in the first prime and outside the second, witnessing failure of the proposed implication. [prop:cyclic-upper](#prop:cyclic-upper) supplies the opposite inequality. Thus $r_n(G,H)=\ell(G,H)$ for all the stated groups, subgroups, and heights. The rational height $n=0$ has been retained throughout, and when $H=G$ both sides are zero. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
#### The general finite-group consequence

For a finite group $J$, let $O^p(J)$ denote its smallest normal subgroup of $p$-power index. We continue to use tested-height indices and work in the $p$-local genuine category.

<a id="stmt-cor-general-groups"></a>
<div id="cor:general-groups" class="corollary">

**Corollary 29**. *Let $G$ be any finite group, $p$ a prime, $H,K\le G$, and $m,n\ge0$. Then $$\mathcal P_m^G(K)\subseteq\mathcal P_n^G(H)$$ if and only if there is a conjugate $K^g=gKg^{-1}\le H$ such that $$N=O^p(H)=O^p(K^g),\qquad
 m\ge n+\ell(H/N,K^g/N).$$ In particular, the numerical test is applied to a qualifying conjugate in the finite $p$-group $H/N$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-general-groups"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-general-groups</summary>

*Proof.* Balmer–Sanders <a id="pm-cite-51"></a>[[BS](#ref-BS), Proposition 6.11] reduce the displayed inclusion to the existence of such a conjugate and the inclusion $\mathcal P_m^{H/N}(K^g/N)\subseteq
\mathcal P_n^{H/N}(H/N)$. Their prime indices are $m+1,n+1$. The common $p$-residual condition is equivalent to $p$-subnormality of $K^g$ in $H$, and $H/N$ is a $p$-group. The relative specialization bound and the cyclic upper bound give precisely the stated inequality. When $m<n$, an inflated finite spectrum of type $m+1$ rules out the reduced inclusion, as in the proof of [thm:specialization](#thm:specialization). For the upper direction at $m>n+\ell(H/N,K^g/N)$, use also the nesting of the Morava-acyclic ideals of finite spectra. ◻

</details>
